The quarrel was never about who Christ is.\n\nWhat we kept nearest was the Christ who stood condemned before the governor and was right. When an imperial judge at Carthage offered our bishops chairs, our own bishop of Constantina refused them. We do not blush, he said, we do not fear, we do not avoid standing while you sit — and since Christ our Lord did not avoid it, when he deigned to stand before the governor, how much less may we. That is the Christ we carried: the one tried by lawful authority, who lost the case, and was right.\n\nThe good news ran along the same line. When the persecution opened, the emperor's edict demanded that the scriptures be handed over and burned, along with the vessels of the church. Some of our clergy carried the codices out and gave them up. Others refused and paid for refusing. We are the church that came out of that difference, and we have never stopped naming it.\n\nThey reached us through Africa, and through Carthage, in Latin, in the line of Cyprian — our own bishop and our own martyr, half a century before the persecution that made us. His letters and his councils were ours before they were anyone else's.\n\nNone of us had known someone who saw him. Three hundred years stood between us and that, and we never pretended otherwise. We did not argue from a chain of eyewitnesses at all. We argued from a chain of hands — whose hands ordained whose, back to a point where the line was still clean.\n\nOn how we knew the resurrection happened, we will not manufacture an answer.             {
              "id": "don.term.martyr-martyrdom",
              "matched_name": "martyr",
              "text_start": 497,
              "plain_meaning": "A martyr dies for the true faith. But our best-known dead were not killed by pagans. They were killed by imperial troops sent at the urging of a rival Christian party. That party did not grant them the word. We did not accept its account of our own dead.",
              "quick_meaning": "Our dead, killed for the true faith -- and the word our rival will not grant them.",
              "translational_sense": "'Wasn't every ancient church proud of its martyrs?' The difference here is who did the killing. We held that we were the true one, and that our rival's favor with the state settled nothing.\n\nWhere it came from is the Latin word for an assembly, taken over by Christians to name the body Christ gathers. But by our day, in Africa, there was no using it without taking a side. When you said ecclesia, you meant your church, not theirs. They said it of themselves too. He stood condemned before the governor and was right.\n\nEvery year, on the day one of our own was killed, we stood at the grave and heard the account of that death read out again. On the twelfth of March we heard how soldiers took a basilica at Carthage in the name of unity and killed people at prayer inside it. That was not history being remembered. That was the communion being formed again in the hearing.\n\nWe did not come to believe through speculative doctrine. We came to believe by doing the rite, hearing the story, and living inside the pressure. You walked to one font and not the other, on a day everyone could see. The account says his virtues are too many to tell, but a few must be told. He had refused worldly advancement before persecution ever came near him. The cliff is the last four days of his story.\n\nHe was held at Novapetra. On the last night, after a day of fasting, he was shown a cup, a crown and a palm. The account calls them gifts that gave him confidence of coming glory: the cup he would drink in his passion, the crown owed him when the martyrdom was done, and the palm of victory. Before dawn he was thrown from the cliff. The tradition says his body came down gently, and a bright cloud showed the brethren where he lay.\n\nThat is the Christ we carried: the one who stood condemned before the governor and was right. The transcript is our own bishops' own words taken down by an imperial notary, which is unusually good ground; the printing it reaches this compilation through carries the worst OCR damage of any text in this corpus, so the wording is carried as reported sense and not as a quotable clause. That we confessed the same creed as our opponents is not our own claim but a finding all sides agree on: the division was called a schism, not a heresy, until Augustine's writing around 400 and the emperor's law of 405, and even then the charge rested on separation and not on any difference of creed.",

A tainted hand gave nothing, so we were washed again, and that washing was our true entry. We remembered our dead by name, at the grave, on the appointed day. And we would not grant the emperor standing to decide which of two churches was the true one. Purity, the washing, the memory of our martyrs and our refusal of the state went together.\n\nThe conviction did not hold without strain. The rule we stated absolutely did not hold everywhere. Our councils received some clergy back without washing or ordaining them again. The refusal we held as principle, we set aside three times when it served us. No account of how we explained this to ourselves survives.\n\nAnd underneath it all: we came to believe not by being taught a doctrine, but by doing the rite, hearing the story, and living inside the pressure. You walked to one font on a day everyone could see. You stood at the grave and heard the account read. You lived under a state that ruled against you. The doctrine explained the pressure.
